MC	<p>5f24_1f80</p> <h3>Phenotype Diversity in Hybrid Cross</h3><p>Aysun and Luke are crossed. Use their genotypes to determine the number of different phenotypes possible in their offspring. An uppercase letter represents a dominant allele; a lowercase letter represents a recessive allele.</p><p><i>Hint: At each gene, Aa &times; Aa and Aa &times; aa can produce two phenotypes. All other crosses produce one phenotype. Multiply the counts across all genes.</i></p><p>Assume complete dominance and the principle of independent assortment for all genes.</p><p>Given these principles, how many unique <span style="color: OrangeRed;"><strong>PHENOTYPES</strong></span> could result from a hybrid cross between the following individuals?</p><p>Use the blank table to organize your work. For each gene, enter the number of possibilities, then multiply across the row to find the total.</p><p>Work in order: (a) and (b) count the different gametes each person can produce; (c) give the Punnett square dimensions (rows &times; columns) and total number of cells; then count the different offspring outcomes in the remaining row(s). You do not need to draw the full Punnett square.</p><p>Fill in the white cells and shaded TOTAL cells. You do not need to draw the full Punnett square.</p><p>Fill in the white cells and shaded TOTAL cells. All other crosses produce one phenotype. Multiply the counts across all genes.</i></p><p>Assume complete dominance and the principle of independent assortment for all genes.</p><p>Given these principles, how many unique <span style="color: OrangeRed;"><strong>PHENOTYPES</strong></span> could result from a hybrid cross between the following individuals?</p><p>Use the blank table to organize your work. You do not need to draw the full Punnett square.</p><p>Fill in the white cells and shaded TOTAL cells. All other crosses produce one phenotype. Multiply the counts across all genes.</i></p><p>Assume complete dominance and the principle of independent assortment for all genes.</p><p>Given these principles, how many unique <span style="color: OrangeRed;"><strong>PHENOTYPES</strong></span> could result from a hybrid cross between the following individuals?</p><p>Use the blank table to organize your work. For each gene, enter the number of possibilities, then multiply across the row to find the total.</p><p>Work in order: (a) and (b) count the different gametes each person can produce; (c) give the Punnett square dimensions (rows &times; columns) and total number of cells; then count the different offspring outcomes in the remaining row(s). You do not need to draw the full Punnett square.</p><p>Fill in the white cells and shaded TOTAL cells. You do not need to draw the full Punnett square.</p><p>Fill in the white cells and shaded TOTAL cells. All other crosses produce one phenotype. Multiply the counts across all genes.</i></p><p>Assume complete dominance and the principle of independent assortment for all genes.</p><p>Given these principles, how many unique <span style="color: OrangeRed;"><strong>PHENOTYPES</strong></span> could result from a hybrid cross between the following individuals?</p><p>Use the blank table to organize your work.
